</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Legislature</th><td class="infobox-data"><span class="nowrap"><a href="/wiki/National_People%27s_Assembly_(Guinea-Bissau)" title="National People&#39;s Assembly (Guinea-Bissau)">National People's Assembly</a></span></td></tr><tr class="mergedtoprow"><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header"><a href="/wiki/Guinea-Bissau_War_of_Independence" title="Guinea-Bissau War of Independence">Independence</a> <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r886047488"/><span class="nobold">from <a href="/wiki/Portugal" title="Portugal">Portugal</a></span></th></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"/></td></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><div style="text-indent:-0.9em;margin-left:1.2em;font-weight:normal;">•&#160;Declared </div></th><td class="infobox-data">24 September 1973</td></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><div style="text-indent:-0.9em;margin-left:1.2em;font-weight:normal;">•&#160;Recognized </div></th><td class="infobox-data">10 September 1974</td></tr><tr class="mergedrow"><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><div style="text-indent:-0.9em;margin-left:1.2em;font-weight:normal;">•&#160;Independence </div></th><td class="infobox-data">5 July 1975</td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">

<p><b>Guinea-Bissau</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˌ/: secondary stress follows">ˌ</span><span title="/ɡ/: &#39;g&#39; in &#39;guy&#39;">ɡ</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/i/: &#39;y&#39; in &#39;happy&#39;">i</span></span><span class="wrap"> </span><span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="&#39;b&#39; in &#39;buy&#39;">b</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;s&#39; in &#39;sigh&#39;">s</span><span title="/aʊ/: &#39;ou&#39; in &#39;mouth&#39;">aʊ</span></span>/</a></span>&#32;<span class="nowrap" style="font-size:85%">(<span class="unicode haudio"><span class="fn"><span style="white-space:nowrap;margin-right:.25em;"><a href="/wiki/File:En-us-Guinea-Bissau.ogg" title="About this sound"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/11px-Loudspeaker.svg.png" decoding="async" width="11" height="11" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/17px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Loudspeaker.svg/22px-Loudspeaker.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="20" data-file-height="20" /></a></span><a href="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/ce/En-us-Guinea-Bissau.ogg" class="internal" title="En-us-Guinea-Bissau.ogg">listen</a></span></span>)</span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling"><span style="font-size:90%">GHIN</span>-ee biss-<span style="font-size:90%">OW</span></i></a>; <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_language" title="Portuguese language">Portuguese</a>: <i lang="pt">Guiné-Bissau</i>; <a href="/wiki/Fula_language" title="Fula language">Fula</a>: <span lang="ff-Adlm" dir="rtl" style="font-style: normal;">𞤘𞤭𞤲𞤫 𞤄𞤭𞤧𞤢𞥄𞤱𞤮</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Fula" class="mw-redirect" title="Romanization of Fula">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Fula-language romanization"><i lang="ff-Adlm-Latn">Gine-Bisaawo</i></span>; <a href="/wiki/Mandinka_language" title="Mandinka language">Mandinka</a>: <span title="Mandinka-language text"><span lang="mnk" dir="rtl">ߖߌߣߍ&#160;ߺ ߓߌߛߊߥߏ߫</span></span> <i>Gine-Bisawo</i>), officially the <b>Republic of Guinea-Bissau</b> (Portuguese: <i lang="pt">República da Guiné-Bissau</i> <span style="font-size:85%;"></span><span title="Representation in the International Phonetic Alphabet (IPA)" class="IPA" lang="pt-Latn-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/Portuguese" title="Help:IPA/Portuguese">[ʁɛˈpuβlikɐ ðɐ ɣiˈnɛ βiˈsaw]</a></span>), is a country in <a href="/wiki/West_Africa" title="West Africa">West Africa</a> that covers 36,125 square kilometres (13,948&#160;sq&#160;mi) with an estimated population of 1,726,000. It borders <a href="/wiki/Senegal" title="Senegal">Senegal</a> to <a href="/wiki/Guinea-Bissau%E2%80%93Senegal_border" title="Guinea-Bissau–Senegal border">the north</a> and <a href="/wiki/Guinea" title="Guinea">Guinea</a> to <a href="/wiki/Guinea%E2%80%93Guinea-Bissau_border" title="Guinea–Guinea-Bissau border">the south-east</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Guinea-Bissau was once part of the kingdom of <a href="/wiki/Kaabu" title="Kaabu">Kaabu</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-nationsonline.org_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nationsonline.org-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> as well as part of the <a href="/wiki/Mali_Empire" title="Mali Empire">Mali Empire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-nationsonline.org_11-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nationsonline.org-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Parts of this kingdom persisted until the 18th century, while a few others were under some rule by the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Empire" title="Portuguese Empire">Portuguese Empire</a> since the 16th century. In the 19th century, it was colonised as <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Guinea" title="Portuguese Guinea">Portuguese Guinea</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-nationsonline.org_11-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-nationsonline.org-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Portuguese control was restricted and weak until the early 20th century with the pacification campaigns, these campaigns solidified Portuguese sovereignty in the area. The final Portuguese victory over the remaining bastion of mainland resistance, the <a href="/wiki/Papel_people" title="Papel people">Papel</a> ruled Kingdom of Bissau in 1915 by the Portuguese military office <a href="/wiki/Jo%C3%A3o_Teixeira_Pinto" title="João Teixeira Pinto">Teixeira Pinto</a>, and recruited Wolof mercenary <a href="/wiki/Abdul_Injai" title="Abdul Injai">Abdul injai</a> was the event to solidify mainland control.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Bissagos_Islands" title="Bissagos Islands">Bissagos</a>, islands off the coast of Guinea-Bissau, were officially conquered in 1936, ensuring Portuguese control of both the mainland and islands  of the region.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Upon independence, declared in 1973 and recognised in 1974, the name of its capital, <a href="/wiki/Bissau" title="Bissau">Bissau</a>, was added to the country's name to prevent confusion with <a href="/wiki/Guinea" title="Guinea">Guinea</a> (formerly <a href="/wiki/French_Guinea" title="French Guinea">French Guinea</a>). Guinea-Bissau has a history of political instability since independence, and only one elected president (<a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_M%C3%A1rio_Vaz" title="José Mário Vaz">José Mário Vaz</a>) has successfully served a full five-year term.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> The current president is <a href="/wiki/Umaro_Sissoco_Embal%C3%B3" title="Umaro Sissoco Embaló">Umaro Sissoco Embaló</a>, who was elected on 29 December 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Only about 2% of the population speaks Portuguese, the official language, as a first language, and 33% speak it as a second language. However, <a href="/wiki/Guinea-Bissau_Creole" title="Guinea-Bissau Creole">Guinea-Bissau Creole</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Portuguese-based_creole_languages" title="Portuguese-based creole languages">Portuguese-based creole</a>, is the national language and also considered the language of unity. According to a 2012 study, 54% of the population speak Creole as a first language and about 40% speak it as a second language.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> The remainder speak a variety of native African languages. The nation is home to numerous followers of <a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a>, <a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a> and <a href="/wiki/Traditional_African_religion" class="mw-redirect" title="Traditional African religion">traditional faiths</a>, though no single religious group represents a majority of the population.<sup id="cite_ref-cia-religion_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cia-religion-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:0_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> The country's per-capita <a href="/wiki/Gross_domestic_product" title="Gross domestic product">gross domestic product</a> is one of the <a href="/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(PPP)_per_capita" title="List of countries by GDP (PPP) per capita">lowest in the world</a>.
</p><p>Guinea-Bissau is a member of the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a>, <a href="/wiki/African_Union" title="African Union">African Union</a>, <a href="/wiki/Economic_Community_of_West_African_States" title="Economic Community of West African States">Economic Community of West African States</a>, <a href="/wiki/Organisation_of_Islamic_Cooperation" title="Organisation of Islamic Cooperation">Organisation of Islamic Cooperation</a>, <a href="/wiki/Community_of_Portuguese_Language_Countries" title="Community of Portuguese Language Countries">Community of Portuguese Language Countries</a>, <a href="/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_Francophonie" title="Organisation internationale de la Francophonie">Organisation internationale de la Francophonie</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/South_Atlantic_Peace_and_Cooperation_Zone" title="South Atlantic Peace and Cooperation Zone">South Atlantic Peace and Cooperation Zone</a>, and was a member of the now-defunct <a href="/wiki/Latin_Union" title="Latin Union">Latin Union</a>.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History">History</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: History">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Guinea-Bissau" title="History of Guinea-Bissau">History of Guinea-Bissau</a> and <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Guinea" title="Portuguese Guinea">Portuguese Guinea</a></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Pre-European_contact">Pre-European contact</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Pre-European contact">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="People_and_society">People and society</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: People and society">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Archaeology has insufficiently explained the Guinea-Bissau pre-history. In 1000 AD, there were hunter-gatherers in the area, hundreds of thousands of years after they traversed the rest of Africa. This was shortly followed, in the archaeological record, by agriculturists using iron tools.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Guinea Bissau has always been connected to the interior, trade routes connected Guinea Bissau to the Sudanese states (p. 18).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Megaliths discovered in Guinea Bissau sharing similarity with rock paintings in the Sahara detailed with horses and camels, and ancient gold workings (p. 19).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The population originated from the interior, pushed coastal by the Sudanese states (p.19, 25).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The oldest inhabitants of were the <a href="/wiki/Jola_people" title="Jola people">Jolas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Papel_people" title="Papel people">Papels</a>, <a href="/wiki/Manjak_people" title="Manjak people">Manjaks</a>, <a href="/wiki/Balanta_people" title="Balanta people">Balantas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Biafada_people" title="Biafada people">Biafadas</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Bissagos_Islands" title="Bissagos Islands">Bijagos</a> later on the <a href="/wiki/Mandinka_people" title="Mandinka people">Mandinka</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fula_people" title="Fula people">Fulani</a> migrated into the region (p. 20).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The Mandinka migrated in mass around the 13th century, result of the invasion and incorporation of <a href="/wiki/Senegambia" title="Senegambia">Senegambia</a> into the <a href="/wiki/Mali_Empire" title="Mali Empire">Mali Empire</a> by General <a href="/wiki/Tiramakhan_Traore" title="Tiramakhan Traore">Tiramakhan Troare</a>, also responsible for the establishment of <a href="/wiki/Kaabu" title="Kaabu">Kaabu</a> as a province of Mali.<sup id="cite_ref-:5_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:5-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> A small number of Mandinka were present in the region as early as the 11th century with hunters, fishermen, and traders migrating into the Senegambia. Accepted by the locals and beginning a process of  'Mandinkization' (p. 6).<sup id="cite_ref-:6_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> The Fulani arrived as early as the 12th century as semi-nomadic herders, in the 15th century their population increased.<sup id="cite_ref-:13_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:13-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Papels inhabited the <a href="/wiki/Biombo_Region" title="Biombo Region">Biombo</a> region, Balanta the <a href="/wiki/Oio_Region" title="Oio Region">Oio</a> region, Manjaks the <a href="/wiki/Cacheu_Region" title="Cacheu Region">Cacheu</a> region, Mandinka overlap in north east Oio but are mainly in the <a href="/wiki/Bafat%C3%A1_Region" title="Bafatá Region">Bafata</a> region, Biafada the <a href="/wiki/Quinara_Region" title="Quinara Region">Quinara</a> region, Bijagos the <a href="/wiki/Bissagos_Islands" title="Bissagos Islands">Bijagos</a>, and Fulani in <a href="/wiki/Gabu_Region" title="Gabu Region">Gabu</a>, though Mandinka and Fulani overlap with each other. (p. 23).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The Manjaks and Papels are intelligible resulting in European sources labelling Manjaks as Buramos/Papels (p. 6).<sup id="cite_ref-:11_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:11-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>In terms of societal structures the Mandinka, Fula, Papel, Manjak, and Biafada possessed highly stratified structures.  The Balanta had no institution of kingship based on property, authority, or power with instead an emphasis on heads of villages and families present. The Jola possessed kings but no clear institutions of kingship, of which mirrored the Balanta with emphasis on heads of families (p. 64).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Those with societal stratification followed the design of provinces with chiefs, and chiefs in allegiance to kings. The Biafada had three kingdoms with multiple chieftains under the king, and Papels in Bissau the same structure (p. 65).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Kingship varied in terms of customs, rites, coronation, and ceremonies (pp. 66, 67).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The Portuguese observed these societies holding courts with officials, such as 'mayors', 'governors', 'ambassadors', and 'generals' (p. 68).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The society was divided into the hierarchy of kings, chiefs, nobles, and commoners (p. 73).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Law of the land was the kings law and administration of the law was done through the king and his judges, judges all coming from the nobility (p. 227).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Those of the nobility were the only ones with the ability to make claim to the throne, or other high positions of power in the kingdoms (pp. 74–76).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Additionally, the stratification was seen in the clothing and accessories of the people with the poor wearing grass skirts, the better off animal skin usually from goatskin, kings and nobles shirts and trousers made of cotton. An iron ring with bells to give commands was worn by kings and nobles, a drum called bombalon used for communication was in the hands of the kings and nobles due to the financing of these specially trained bombalon players. Differences were seen in the houses with better construction material available for kings and nobles. The few horses there were owned by kings and nobles, while common people used castrated oxen for transportation (pp. 77, 78).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Private property was instituted amongst the Mandinka, Fula, Papel, Manjaks, and Biafadas in Guinea Bissau. However, the Balanta were distinct in that the Balantas owned parcels of land, labour was through the use of reciprocation through the reward of land they worked on. The others contrasted the Balanta, as land development was in the hands of those who could afford the necessary labour, and land was not administered to labourers as a form of payment (p. 75).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>  
</p><p>Inter-ethnic relations was mainly in trade, the Balantas possessed the best agricultural techniques, leading the Beafada and Papels to be reliant on Balanta produce. Balanta unwillingness to trade with Europeans meant goods reached Europeans through the Papel and Beafada and vice-versa (p. 69).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Items traded amongst the region were as such, Biafadas traded pepper and kola nuts from the southern forests, the Papel, Felupe, and Banhun traded kola nuts, iron, and iron utensils from the savannah-forest zone, coastal regions provided salt and dried fish, the Mandinka themselves produced cotton cloth (p. 4).<sup id="cite_ref-:7_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:7-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> The products were commonly sold on markets and fairs held weekly acting as both commercial and social functions, held usually in the lands of the Papel and Beafada open to the public on certain days of the week or every eight days (p. 69).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Markets typically opened up on the morning of the day, and finished in the evening to be repeated the week after, weapons were prohibited in the market place and soldiers were positioned around the area to keep order throughout the day (p. 70).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Markets and fairs could be attended by up to several thousand buyers and sellers from as far as 60 miles away, the organisation went as such, sections of the market would be allocated for specific products except for wine which could be sold anywhere, the exception for wine vendors highlighted the markets social function (p.70).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>A culture of naval navigation was integral due the river systems of Guinea Bissau, canoes were routinely used to navigate riverways. The canoes importance were highlighted in a local proverb used in the region: "the blood of kings and the tears of the canoe-maker are sacred things which must not touch the ground" (pp. 42–44).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Canoes varied in size with the smallest fit for one man, to those that could carry 60 men, in the region only Bijago canoes were deemed sea-worthy (pp. 42, 43).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The Jolas used one man canoes to travel their flooded rice fields, and the Bijagos preferred large canoes to carry their troops to the mainland in coastal raids (p. 42).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The construction of canoes varied in the region, in the forest regions canoes tended to be imported from other ethnical groups who had specialties in its construction, the Jola would employ Mandinka boatbuilders for theirs (p. 43).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>Description of the canoes used in this region was made by British explorer <a href="/wiki/John_Hawkins_(naval_commander)" title="John Hawkins (naval commander)">John Hawkins</a>: 
</p><p>"Fashioned from a single trunk the final proportions were 24 x 3 feet, with a prow in the form of a beak, a proportionately raised stem, and an exterior artistically carved and painted blue. Each held about twenty to thirty men, but the active crew comprised a helmsman and four rowers, using very long oars with relatively small blades," (p. 42).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The construction and design of these canoes was diverse, the Bijagos being the best canoes were described as: 
</p><p>"hewn from the giant silk cotton tree, and measuring about seventy feet in length. A number of boards, called falsas by the Portuguese, were added to the sides, and, thus modified, each almadia (Canoe) carried twenty-four men and their weapons, and had room for prisoners and cattle when returning from their expeditions on the mainland." (p. 42).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The technique of rowing employed by the Bijagos was described as:  
</p><p>"It was also propelled in a different manner from the canoes lower down the coast in Sierra Leone, according to a nineteenth century reference. All the individuals on board were rowers, who squatted at the bottom of the boat, and rose at the beginning of each stroke of their short oars," (p. 43).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Kingdom_of_Bissau_.281300.E2.80.931915.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Kingdom_of_Bissau_(1300–1915)">Kingdom of Bissau (1300–1915)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Kingdom of Bissau (1300–1915)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Origins">Origins</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Origins">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>The Kingdom of Bissau was started by the son of the King of Quinara, who began the kingdom when he moved to Bissau with his pregnant sister, six wives, and subjects from his father's kingdom.<sup id="cite_ref-:8_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:8-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> The seven clans of the kingdom are said to have come from the sister and six wives of Mecau, these being the Bottat, Bossuzu, Boiga, Bosafinte, Bodjukumo, Bosso, and Bossassun, the latter of whom descends from the sister of Mecau. Characteristic of the region, the Bossassun inherited the throne, and were the nobility alongside the Bodjukumo.<sup id="cite_ref-:8_26-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:8-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> The Kingdom of Bissau had multiple vassal states such as Prabis, Antula, Safim, Quisset, Tor, and Biombo.
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Society">Society</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Society">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>The Kingdom of Bissau was highly stratified with the top of society being the king, nobles, and then commoners and was strictly enforced (pp. 73–79).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>The King of Bissau would go through their coronation, receive their badge of office for this kingdom was a spear, other Papel Kingdoms used the bow (p. 66).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The coronation involved the practice of binding and beating the king, as the king should know what punishment felt like before administering it (p. 66).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Nobles would be assigned to principalities as governors subject to the King of Bissau and part of his court (p. 364).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Houses in the kingdom were made of clay, and roofs of leaves from the surrounding trees, and the inhabitants were pagans until the Jesuits arrival (p. 366).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>  
</p><p>Papels suffered slave raids from the Bijagos who would stage maritime expeditions into Bissau for slaves (p. 204).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> However, Papels themselves were slave traders, staging slave raids against the Balantas, Biafadas, and Bijagos with the assistance from the Europeans and <a href="/wiki/Lan%C3%A7ados" title="Lançados">Lançados</a> (p. 207).<sup id="cite_ref-:13_23-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:13-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>   
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Decline">Decline</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Decline">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>Centuries of warfare between the Kingdom of Bissau and Portuguese Empire of which the kingdom strongly defended its sovereignty, defeating the Portuguese in the years of 1891, 1894, and 1904 during the Pacification Campaigns (p. 9).<sup id="cite_ref-:3_12-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> However, in 1915 and after 30 years of the Portuguese campaigns, the Portuguese defeated the Kingdom of Bissau under the command of Officer <a href="/wiki/Jo%C3%A3o_Teixeira_Pinto" title="João Teixeira Pinto">Teixeira Pinto</a>, and Warlord <a href="/wiki/Abdul_Injai" title="Abdul Injai">Abdul Injai</a>, and for the first time in the kingdoms existence lost its independence.<sup id="cite_ref-:3_12-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:3-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Beafada_Kingdoms">Beafada Kingdoms</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Beafada Kingdoms">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Kingdom_of_Guinala">Kingdom of Guinala</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Kingdom of Guinala">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>The King of Guinala was a grandiose figure, attended too by a retinue of archers, alongside 50 guard dogs dressed in tough Sea-Cow skin  (p. 365).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> These dogs were formed in response to slave raiders breaking into homes, and kidnapping them (p. 365).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Under the king there were seven governors who wore hats given to them as a sign of their station (p. 365).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> The kingdom extended jurisdiction over six kingdoms, the governors of these kingdoms were gathered in the form of a council.  Under the king would be the main governor who was described as a President (p. 365).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Their religion was <a href="/wiki/Idolatry" title="Idolatry">idol worship</a>. The idols in the region were called 'Xina', though some converted to Roman Catholicism in the early period of European contact (p. 366).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Royal funerals went as such, twelve men in long coats made of feathers, following a band of pipers playing mournful music, declared the kings death to the masses on the streets (p. 366).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> White clothes would be worn them for the day, nothing being done except walking the streets in mourning. The late kings friends, relatives, and servants would  congregate to appoint a successor (p. 366).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> The kings body would be washed, his entrails burnt before an idol, the ashes of his entrails preserved and put with the body of which would lie in state for a month, then all the subjects of the kingdoms would bring <a href="/wiki/Balsam" title="Balsam">balsam</a>, <a href="/wiki/Myrrh" title="Myrrh">myrrh</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ambergris" title="Ambergris">ambergris</a>, <a href="/wiki/Musk" title="Musk">musk</a>, and other perfumes to burn and be smoked around the corps (p. 366).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Six eminent people would carry the body to be buried while clothed in white silk, behind a band of musicians followed with mournful music, who in turn are followed by many people singing mournfully or crying aloud, the prince followed on horseback while dressed in white (p. 366).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Near the grave waited his women, servants, horses, and favourite people to be put to death and buried alongside him, to serve in the afterlife, their death would be by their toes and fingers being severed, and bones crushed by stamping. Servants would try leave the services of the king prior to his death, or hide when they realised he would not recover (p. 366).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Kingdom_of_Biguba">Kingdom of Biguba</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Kingdom of Biguba">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>The people of the kingdom lived the same way as those from the Kingdom of Guinala. Observers to this kingdom said once a king died, the crown fell went to the strongest family, this would and did lead to armed conflict, battles lasted until the strongest contestant reduced his opponent to obedience (p. 367).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>They followed the same religion as those from the Kingdom of Guinala (p. 367).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The Kingdom of Biguba had less chiefs underneath them than the Kingdom of Guinala, holding administration over four kingdoms with four governors (p. 65).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A sizeable population of Afro-Portuguese citizens lived in the kingdom, these Afro-Portuguese swore allegiance to the natives, and they would follow local religions, dress like the locals, and undergo scarification (p. 366).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Island_States_of_the_Bijagos">Island States of the Bijagos</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Island States of the Bijagos">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Origins_2">Origins</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Origins">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>The Bijagos are from the region the Biafada are currently in, leaving their original home for the Islands, the population that migrated were not homogenous with Islands having differing affinities to different mainland ethnicities (p. 25).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Each Island was governed by lords whom swore allegiance to the King of Isla do Po (p. 364).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> All the Islands were inhabited except for the Island of Bolama which was inhabited by the Biafadas, however, control of the Island has traded hands multiple times (p. 7)<sup id="cite_ref-:11_24-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:11-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> (p. 5).<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Society_2">Society</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Society">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>Described as large in stature, and reputed for their courage and hardy disposition, they were renowned for their skills in boat craft, sailing, harassing the waters of the region, and regular staging of raids on the mainland. The Bijagos would attack European ships that crossed their waters, and those who tried to take their land (p. 364).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>  The Bijago canoes were unique in that they were sea worthy, meaning after their coastal raids regions they did not have to fear retaliation. 
</p><p>Bijago society was warlike. Women cultivated land, constructed houses, and gathered food (p. 204).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Men were dedicated to boat crafting and warring the mainland, attacking the coastal people like the Jolas, Papels, and Balantas, and believing on the sea they had no king, also attacking other Islands (p. 204).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> These soldiers were reputed to be excellent swimmers, sailors, and soldiers, in this society women chose their husbands, only choosing warriors with the biggest reputation. Successful warriors could have many wives and boats, the owner of these boats were entitled to 1/3 of the spoils of any expedition (p. 205).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>  
</p><p>Coastal raids went as such, warriors anointing their body in red ochre, coal and white clay, placed feathers in their hair, and hung horse tails from their breasts with little bells. A priestess would break an egg over the stern of the boat, setting off to arrive on the coast at night (p. 205).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> With rapid speed they would arrive on the coast, surroun coastal villages, set fire to the homes, and if met with resisted would be cut down though occupants usually surrendered (p. 205).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> So efficient were these raids that Portuguese traders tried to get the Portuguese authorities to stop them, as they were decimating the Biafadas, however, so successful were these raids, the Portuguese were getting a high surplus of slaves, and Lemos Coelho a Cape Verdean trader described how in 25 trips over a couple of years over a 1,000 slaves were captured by the raids (p. 206).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> In the early 17th century with the monetary gains made from the raids, the Bijago Islands joined together in their war with the mainland, increasing the size of their fleets and soldiers (p. 206).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> These raids resulted in the King of Guinala losing six kingdoms, and the king fleeing into the forest (p. 364).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> The Portuguese influence on the Bijago was by appealing to their honour, if slaves were few in number in the ports they would call it a stain on their good name, and other Europeans would ignore their ports as a result. These appeals to their pride would be enough to increase their raiding intensity (p. 207).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>  
</p><p>The Bijagos were the most safe from enslavement, their lsland kept them out of the hands of the mainland slave raiders (p. 218).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Europeans did not find them fit for slavery and avoided having them as slaves. Portuguese sources say the children made good slaves but not the adults, whom were likely too commit suicide as they believed their spirits returned back to the Bijagos, known for slave rebellions on ships, and in the <a href="/wiki/New_World" title="New World">New World</a> for their tendency to escape (pp. 218, 219).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Kaabu_Province_of_Imperial_Mali_.281200.E2.80.931537.29_.E2.80.93_Kaabu_Empire_.281537.E2.80.931865.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Kaabu_Province_of_Imperial_Mali_(1200–1537)_–_Kaabu_Empire_(1537–1865)">Kaabu Province of Imperial Mali (1200–1537) – Kaabu Empire (1537–1865)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Kaabu Province of Imperial Mali (1200–1537) – Kaabu Empire (1537–1865)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Origins_3">Origins</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Origins">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p><a href="/wiki/Kaabu" title="Kaabu">Kaabu</a> was established as a province of <a href="/wiki/Mali_Empire" title="Mali Empire">Mali</a> through the conquest of the <a href="/wiki/Senegambia" title="Senegambia">Senegambia</a> by the one of the generals of <a href="/wiki/Sundiata_Keita" title="Sundiata Keita">Sundiata Keita</a> called <a href="/wiki/Tiramakhan_Traore" title="Tiramakhan Traore">Tiramakhan Troare</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:9_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:9-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> According to oral tradition Tiramakhan went to the region in retaliation for an insult given to Sundiata by the Wolof King, resulting in the conquest of the Wolof's, and then carried down past the River Gambia into the <a href="/wiki/Casamance" title="Casamance">Casamance</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:9_29-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:9-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> This initiated a migration of Mandinka into the region in the 13th century, though a small population of Mandinka already lived there.<sup id="cite_ref-:9_29-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:9-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> The 14th century a lot of Guinea Bissau was under Mali administration and ruled by a Farim Kaabu (Commander of Kaabu).<sup id="cite_ref-:9_29-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:9-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The decline of the Mali Empire in the 14th century lead to Kaabu becoming independent in the 16th century.<sup id="cite_ref-:10_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:10-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> The right to rule came from their history as an imperial province, Farim Kaabu was replaced with Kaabu Mansaba.<sup id="cite_ref-:10_30-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:10-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> The capital of the empire was Kansala, modern day <a href="/wiki/Gabu_(town)" title="Gabu (town)">Gabu</a>, eastern Guinea Bissau in the <a href="/wiki/Gabu_Region" title="Gabu Region">Geba</a> region (p. 4).<sup id="cite_ref-:7_25-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:7-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> All the region excluding the Papels, Manjaks, and western Biafadas obeyed and paid dues to the Mansaba (p. 367).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Society_3">Society</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Society">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>The empire ran more militaristic with stricter social stratifications than the Mali Empire. The ruling classes were composed of elite warriors from the Nyancho ( Ñaanco) tracing their patrilineal lineage to Tiramakhan Troare, and matrilineal to a mysterious native called Baleba who was believed to possess supernatural powers (p. 3).<sup id="cite_ref-:5_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:5-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> The Mansaba was established matrilineally through the sister of the Mansaba, whose son would be next in line (p. 3).<sup id="cite_ref-:5_21-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:5-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>  
</p><p>The Nyancho were immersed in a <a href="/wiki/Warrior_Culture" class="mw-redirect" title="Warrior Culture">warrior culture</a>, and were reputed to be excellent cavalry men and warring raiders, slaves farmed and maintained their mounts, for the Nyancho the highest honour was to achieve Mansaba (p. 6).<sup id="cite_ref-:6_22-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Young men would travel solely for the goal of marauding and war. Europeans reported these were expert horsemen to the point other kingdoms requested them, and they typically filled the high military ranks of other forces (p. 369).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The society was militaristic focused on control of the slave trade in the region. The warrior clans became rich trading with the Europeans (p. 6).<sup id="cite_ref-:6_22-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> In efforts to stave off northern military incursions from the Serer and Wolof states, and control trade they incorporated surrounding states, giving better protect to themselves and securing economic gain from the trade markets (p. 7)<sup id="cite_ref-:6_22-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>.Trade would bring the noble spirits, imported cloth, beads, metal ware, and firearms as commodities that enhanced their prestige, and a surplus of foodstuffs providing security and securing political alliances (p. 8).<sup id="cite_ref-:6_22-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> The life maintained by the elites had to of course be sustained, this is where commoners and slaves became useful, the latter maintained horses and did the agricultural work, commoners were the farmers, artisans created farm equipment and tools for horse riding, and the marabouts and non-Islamic priests dealt in magic and divination (p. 8).<sup id="cite_ref-:6_22-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> In summary the Empires organisation was that the central government was in Kansala, the Kaabu Mansaba was the emperor with Farim Mansa's as governors of each province, provinces provided soldiers and were further divided into administrative units, who were governed over by aristocratic families (p. 5).<sup id="cite_ref-:7_25-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:7-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The empires was Mandinka, the lingua franca was Mandinka, the social institutions was Mandinka, the political institutions was Mandinka, and the historical traditions was Mandinka, the empire prided itself on its Imperial Mandinka history (p. 11).<sup id="cite_ref-:6_22-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> 'Mandinkization' was big in the empire, individuals from other ethnic background became Mandinka culturally, and the frequent inter-ethnic marriages between the Mandinka and other ethnicities assisted the process, Europeans and Afro-Europeans living in the region could and would become 'Mandinkized' to a certain extent, due to these loose ethnical boundaries it became that kinship was more important than ethnicity (p. 12).<sup id="cite_ref-:6_22-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Kinship was important at the highest echelons of society. The elite were likely to identify with each other regardless of ethnic backgrounds, <i>Soninke</i> who practiced <i>Soninkeya</i> was their identification, <i>Soninke</i> referred to animists in the region, this term bound together these elites regardless of origins or location (p. 12).<sup id="cite_ref-:6_22-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Their religion was the worship of stocks and stones and regularly communication with divinatory, the high priest had residence in the main capital of the empire (p. 368).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Kaabu was the most powerful Western Mandinka state at the time after Mali fell (p. 13).<sup id="cite_ref-:6_22-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>Commoners lived through the employment of their skills, such as growing crops, rearing livestock, becoming traders for the nobles, or marabouts making magical charms for elite warriors (p. 15).<sup id="cite_ref-:6_22-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Those with no way of employing their skills were in a dangerous situation, and servitude was likely to their end under Europeans, North Africans, or far off African courts (p. 15).<sup id="cite_ref-:6_22-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:6-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>   
</p><p>Moreover, the Kaabu integrated more closely the trade networks of Guinea Bissau to North Africa in the 14th century, and the Europeans in the 15th century (p. 3).<sup id="cite_ref-:7_25-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:7-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> The trade that Kaabu tapped into in Guinea Bissau was economically enrichening (p. 4).<sup id="cite_ref-:7_25-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:7-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Slaves were a large source of income, reports estimated in the years between the 1600s and 1700s, 700 slaves annually left the region, so 70,000 slaves in a 100 years were exported, which Kaabu would have had a big hand in supplying (p. 5)<sup id="cite_ref-:7_25-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:7-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> The Arabians (North Africans) and surrounding merchants were noted for trading specifically in the region for gold, which the country was said to have much of (p. 367).<sup id="cite_ref-:12_27-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:12-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h5><span class="mw-headline" id="Decline_2">Decline</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Decline">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h5>
<p>After 800 years of existence and 47 Mansa's the Empire began to decline for multiple reasons including civil war. In the 18th and 19th centuries Muslim states surrounded this pagan state, resulting in the <a href="/wiki/Imamate_of_Futa_Jallon" title="Imamate of Futa Jallon">Imamate of Futa Jallon</a> declaring Jihad on them, with assistance from Muslim <a href="/wiki/Soninke_people" title="Soninke people">Soninke</a> and Mandinka chiefs. Futa Jallon gained support from the local Fula's who wanted independence from Kaabu (pp. 5, 6).<sup id="cite_ref-:7_25-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:7-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>  The two states warred for a number of years with Kaabu repelling the Imamate for a long period, stopping the Imamate at the fort of Berekolong until the 1860s, where they were defeated at Berekolong.<sup id="cite_ref-:10_30-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:10-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> This war lead to a final confrontation between the Imamate and Kaabu in 1867 called the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Kansala" title="Battle of Kansala">Battle of Kansala</a>, an army led by General Alfa Molo Balde laid siege to the earthen walls of Kansala for 11 days.<sup id="cite_ref-:10_30-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:10-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> The Fulani forces consisted of 35,000 ground troops and 12,000 cavalry.<sup id="cite_ref-:10_30-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:10-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> Oral traditions say a Timbo marabout told the Fulani forces that if they fired the first shot they would lose, and a <a href="/wiki/Jakhanke" title="Jakhanke">Jakhanke</a> told the Nyancho if they fired the first shot they would lose.<sup id="cite_ref-:10_30-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:10-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> The siege was in a stalemate until a Nyancho angered at the presence of Fula troops outside their walls, seeing it cowardly not to attack shot first, the Mandinka kept the Fulani from climbing the walls for a time, but the walls were overwhelmed.<sup id="cite_ref-:10_30-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:10-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> The Mansaba Dianke Walli seeing that he would lose, gave the Imamate a pyrrhic victory, ordering his troops to set the cities gunpowder on fire, killing the Mandinka defenders alongside the Imamate ones.<sup id="cite_ref-:10_30-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:10-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> The loss of Kansala marked the end of the Kaabu, alongside Mandinka dominance in the region with their incorporation into the Imamate of Futa Jallon (p. 3).<sup id="cite_ref-:5_21-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:5-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Smaller Mandinka states did continue to exist in the region until their incorporation into the Portuguese Empire (p. 7).<sup id="cite_ref-:7_25-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:7-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="European_contact">European contact</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: European contact">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span id="15th.E2.80.9316th_centuries"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="15th–16th_centuries">15th–16th centuries</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: 15th–16th centuries">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Guinea-Bissau was made known to Europeans by the mid-1400s by mainly Portuguese explorers: Venetian explorer <a href="/wiki/Alvise_Cadamosto" title="Alvise Cadamosto">Alvise Cadamosto</a> in 1455, Portuguese explorer <a href="/wiki/Diogo_Gomes" title="Diogo Gomes">Diogo Gomes</a> in 1456, Portuguese explorer <a href="/wiki/Duarte_Pacheco_Pereira" title="Duarte Pacheco Pereira">Duarte Pacheco Pareira</a> in the 1480s, and Flemish explorer <a href="/wiki/Eustache_de_la_Fosse" title="Eustache de la Fosse">Eustache de la Fosse</a> in 1479–1480 (pp. 7, 12, 13, 16).<sup id="cite_ref-:14_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:14-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Additionally, the region was mentioned in secondary sources like the writings of <a href="/wiki/Gomes_Eanes_de_Zurara" title="Gomes Eanes de Zurara">Gomes Eanes de Zurara</a> in the 1450s, <a href="/wiki/Valentim_Fernandes" title="Valentim Fernandes">Valentim Fernandes</a> in the early 1500s, and German scholar Jerome Münzer  in the early 1500s. (pp. 4, 9, 15).<sup id="cite_ref-:14_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:14-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The region from at least 1550 was known to the Portuguese as 'The Guinea of Cape Verde', <a href="/wiki/Santiago,_Cape_Verde" title="Santiago, Cape Verde">Santiago</a> was the administrative capital of the region (p. 138).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Additionally, most of the settlers in Guinea-Bissau at the time were whites of Cape Verdean origin (p. 139).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Trade between European and African agents began as early as the 1440s (p. 151).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>White settlements on the mainland was discouraged by Portuguese authorities, in contrast to the offshore Islands where settlements were encouraged. This discouragement was ignored by the <a href="/wiki/Lan%C3%A7ados" title="Lançados">Lançados</a> (p. 140).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Tangomão were white trader who assimilated into indigenous culture and customs, also used to describe white traders native auxiliaries (p. 141).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Allied to the Lançados were the 'Grumete' made up of different natives in the region, these were at first slaves of sailors but later began to apply to free native assistants. Assistants varied on the rationale for their placement, some were slaves, others employed, and some relatives to the European trader. Grumetes remained an important part of the Lançados community (p. 151).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Lançados were mainly from impoverished backgrounds, most traders were from Cape Verde and were people exiled from Portugal, traders were also of Jewish and <a href="/wiki/New_Christian" title="New Christian">New Christians</a> backgrounds a result of anti-Semitism in the Iberian peninsula (pp. 148, 150).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Lançados were targeted by Portuguese authorities as they ignored rules and regulations, rules such as the illegality of entering the region without a royal licence, trading goods without being a licenced trader, ships only porting at authorised ports, captains were responsible for their crews, and people regardless of rank could not assimilate into the native community (p. 142).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> In the 1500s Portugal took steps to reduce the Lançado problem in the region, aiming legislations at Cape Verde as the Islands acted like recruitment centres. Rules were made to reduce the chances of recruitment or the carrying out of illegal trade. The legislations were ineffective and did not reduce illegal trading with the mainland (p. 144).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> In 1520 measures against the Lançados reduced, trade and settlements increased on the mainland (p. 145).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Reasons for reduced measures were religious and commercial, and in the latter half of the 1500s no hostile legislation passed against the Lançados (pp. 145, 146).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The settlement population increased on the mainland and it was normal for Europeans to be living side by side with the natives. These Europeans were mainly Portuguese but there were also Spanish, <a href="/wiki/Genoa" title="Genoa">Genoese</a>, English, French, and Dutch settlers in the region (p. 150).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The Lançados were focused on slavery and as intermediaries between European vessels and African producers, the regions rivers possessed no natural harbours, leaving the Lançados to navigate riverways and creeks in small boats, to take native products back to European vessels on the few ports available, being Cacheu, Bissau, and Guinala. The waterways being the main mode of transport, small boats were used by both the Lançados and native to navigate these rivers. Lançados bought boats from European vessels, or in most circumstances built them themselves through specially trained grumetes. At different estuaries trading depots were established some linked to Cape Verde, and others serving as clearing houses for the trading centres of Cacheu, Guinala, and Bissau. The furthest point of the rivers held trading centres connected to the produce of the interior (pp. 153–157).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Settlements were established in relation to these trading centres, convenient villages and locations were chosen for settlement. Villages that had a Lançado population attracted other Africans. Movement in the areas of Bissau, Cacheu, Guinala, and local trading centres were one type of migration because of Portuguese activity, the other was groups from the interior migrating closer to the coast in small numbers, Interior expeditions by the Portuguese were more common than migration. The Mande from the interior offered resources like gums, ivory, hides, civet, dyes, slaves, and gold of which Lançados traversed into the interior for, transporting it back to the coast to be made available to the international community (pp. 158–160).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Trade was not limited to the Atlantic as Lançados helped establish trade between different regions of the <a href="/wiki/Upper_Guinea" title="Upper Guinea">Upper Guinea</a>, trade was opened from the Gambia through Guinea-Bissau to the <a href="/wiki/Grand_Cape_Mount_County" title="Grand Cape Mount County">Cape Mount</a> region (pp. 162, 163).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Europeans were not accepted in all communities, the Jolas, Balantas, and at first the Bijagos were hostile, in the 1500s a common sarcastic saying used by the Papels was:
</p><p>"If you do not like it here, then perhaps the Balantas or the Bijagos would be more to your liking."
</p><p>The other ethnicities of the region interacted with Europeans friendlier, all the groups harboured communities of Lançados. Lançados primarily interacted with the higher echelons of the regions society, any European trader on the land of a native king would be subject to taxation (p. 164, 165).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Moreover, Lançados were subject to the laws and customs of the community they lived in, he was also subject to the local courts. The Lançados would use these local customs to their advantage, pushing to swear oaths and secure deals in the native way to have 100% guarantee (pp. 172, 173).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Latter half of the 1500s the Lançados and their agreement to follow local customs came into question, their attempts to change certain customs and rules caused disagreements, such as in 1580 when they abandoned the settlement of Buguendo near Cacheu, once again in 1583 their settlement in Guinala, and Cacheu where they felt the customs were causing them harm and decided to create fortified settlements of their own along the coast. The forts construction was granted by the King Chapala of Cacheu in 1589 (pp. 175–177). 
</p><p>These changes led to Papels, Manjaks, and Biafadas becoming hostile towards the Portuguese, other reasons for the declining relations was that the natives became fluent in Kriol, and becoming familiar with Lançados for some reason became contemptuous of them (p. 177, 178).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> In 1591 the first significant hostility between the Portuguese and mainland natives occurred, the Manjaks of Cacheu invaded the Fort of Cacheu which was a failure, the conflict ended in a peace agreement. This violent reaction contrasted the Biafadas reaction to the Portuguese actions in 1583, the Biafadas began to charge more for slaves and resources (pp. 179, 180).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> These responses by the native resulted in Lançados withdrawing into their own forts, to defend from any future attacks, and find exemption from native rules and customs. A lack of manpower meant garrisoning the forts of Cacheu and Guinala was difficult, and so they were still subject to the local rules and customs. Kicking the Lançados out was out of the question, as goods that the Lançados brought in were in demand with the upper class natives (pp. 180–184).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The Portuguese were still dealing with the Lançados, whom not hunted like in the early 1500s, still defied authorities in the region causing difficulties with the Portuguese receiving their revenues from the trade, and governing Cape Verde and Guinea Bissau (p. 242).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> In 1580 because of the <a href="/wiki/Iberian_Union" title="Iberian Union">Iberian Union</a> possessions in Guinea Bissau and Cape Verde were attacked by the enemies of Spain. The French, Dutch, and English ships made Portuguese monopoly difficult, and trade increased between other Europeans and the natives. The Lançados would facilitate trade with the English, French, and Dutch in the late 1500s especially at Cacheu, and Cacheu was the centre of Portuguese trade in the region (pp. 244–253).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> 
</p>
<h4><span id="17th.E2.80.9318th_centuries"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="17th–18th_centuries">17th–18th centuries</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: 17th–18th centuries">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In the 1500s, Upper Guinea was split into two districts: the 'Sierra Leone' and 'Rivers of Guinea' with the region of Guinea Bissau falling into the latter. In 1637, the contract of Cape Verde-Guinea was tied to three clauses that gives insight into the regional administration, from Santiago at least 12 ships in a four year period must be sent to the coast, prohibitions on certain items being sold to the natives (Primarily weapons), and guaranteeing settlers free trade on the mainland, along with enough slaves being imported for their own use (pp. 243, 244).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Santiago's importance in the 1600s was reflected in the custom of all ships heading to and from the mainland, stopping and paying duties at Santiago. Santiago stopped ships from Spain and the <a href="/wiki/Canary_Islands" title="Canary Islands">Canaries</a> going to the mainland, and heading to the Spanish Indies and not paying duties, in 1608 and 1615 Cape Verdeans petitioned to further enforce the Santiago rule that was being ignored, Portugal in 1619 reaffirmed the earlier decree of that ships must go through Santiago but was ineffective.  After 1640 the Portuguese fought European encroachment in the region of Cape Verde-Guinea Bissau, in the form of commercial attacks in Cacheu. Portugal had to re-establish monopoly in the region, that had been weakened during the Iberian Union (pp. 254–261).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="Collapse_of_the_Iberian_Union_.281640.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Collapse_of_the_Iberian_Union_(1640)">Collapse of the Iberian Union (1640)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Collapse of the Iberian Union (1640)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The main centres of Portuguese activity after the Iberian Unions collapse in 1640 was Bissau and Cacheu (p. 260).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> An independent Portugal tried to counter Spain's influence, King <a href="/wiki/John_IV_of_Portugal" title="John IV of Portugal">João IV</a> decreed Cape Verde-Guinea Bissau could not trade with Spain, Spanish vessels had to go through the Portuguese authorities, and Spanish ships were embargoed in the ports. These impositions on Spanish vessels were precursors to Portuguese conflicts with the natives and Lançados. From 1580 to 1640, the Spanish were key traders in the region, so Portuguese legislation against Spain affected the natives and pro-Spanish Lançados (pp. 261, 262).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1641, settlers in Bissau, Guinala, Geba, and Cacheu swore allegiance to the Portuguese King and aired grievances stemming from Spanish refusal to trade because of the embargo, further grieved by the 1639 to 1641 famines caused by locust attacks. Spanish trade resumed in 1641, welcomed by the Lançados and Manjak chiefs at Cacheu. The Manjaks who controlled the Cacheu port, viewed European merchandise as necessities, the Spanish not trading caused the Manjaks to threaten the white settlers of Cacheu with death if Captain-Major Luis de Magalhães  did not allow free trade (p. 263).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The settlement at Cacheu was not allowed a water supply leaving the supply of water in Manjak hands, if cut off then trouble would ensue, additionally the famine of 1639–1641 wiped out the slave population in charge of defending the settlement, these and the fact Manjaks surrounded the ports once a ship was spotted to facilitate free trade, pushed Captain-Major Luis de Magalhães to life the embargo and allow free trade against the wishes of the authorities. Free trade in the region between the Spanish, Bissau, Geba, and Cacheu continued regardless of the Portuguese ban, through the facilitation of the Lançados and Manjak, Papel, and Biafada chiefs (pp. 263–265).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The position of Captain-Major was decided by the Conselho Ultramarino, and in 1641 Luis de Magalhães stepped down and was replaced with Gonçalo de Gamboa de Ayala, and Paulo Barradas da Silva was made treasurer. The Portuguese goal was to turn the fort of Cacheu to a fortress of stone, for better protection against European and the natives. Ayala won over the local King of Mata, Chief of Mompatas, Chief of Baorilla, and stopped Spanish ships at Cacheu. The peaceful approach taken was questioned after negotiations with the King Equendé of Bissau, two Spanish ships entered Bissau and were afforded full protection, Ayala for the first time pushed for violent repercussions against the King of Bissau though nothing came of it. Another problem was getting the Lançados under control, he was successfully through resettling the settlers of the infamous settlement of Geba, to the settlement of Farim north of Cacheu taking back control of the rivers estuary, and counter illegal commerce in the region (pp. 266–271).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Successful as these attempts were at  monopolising control in the region, the British, French, and Dutch were still making profits off the Portuguese and native traders (p. 277).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Free trade was difficult for the Portuguese to deal with, as the economic interests of the native leaders and Lançados never fully aligned with the Portuguese. 
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Slave_trade">Slave trade</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Slave trade">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>The slave trade in the region was not as prominent as it was in other regions; however, Guinea-Bissau was a significant region in the Trans-Atlantic slave trade (p. 186).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Slaves at the start were mainly sent to <a href="/wiki/Cape_Verde" title="Cape Verde">Cape Verde</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula" title="Iberian Peninsula">Iberian Peninsula</a>, moreover, the Madeira and Canary Islands saw an influx of Bissau Guinean slaves at lower volumes (p. 187).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> From 1580 to 1640 slaves from Guinea-Bissau were destined for the Spanish Indies (p. 278).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> There were five main ways slaves were made, as punishment for law breaking, selling themselves or relatives during famines, kidnapped by native marauders or European raiders, previously a slave sold to Europeans by a previous master, or as prisoners of war (pp. 198, 199, 210, 211, 216).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Europeans rarely risked slave raiding, the selling of themselves into slavery was most rare, and commonly slaves were bought by Europeans from local rulers or traders (pp. 199, 200).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>  
</p><p>The diverse number of ethnicities made the slave trade common in the region, wars produced prisoners that could be sold off, however, most wars were waged for the sole purpose of capturing slaves (p. 204).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The Bijagos mirrored the latter reason, staging coastal raids to capture slaves, the victims of the Bijagos were the Biafadas, Papels, Jolas, and Balantas (p. 204).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The Papels slave raided the Balantas, Biafadas, and Bijagos with the direct assistance of the Lançados (p. 209).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The Mandinka had great political and military power in the region, in the form of Kaabu Empire, slave raided the coastal groups (pp. 219–221).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The Biafadas were singled out for the amount of criminals sold to Europeans, neighbouring groups accused them of introducing slavery (p. 217).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>  The Balantas and Jolas did not slave raid and were hostile towards it (p. 208, 217).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> In the Jolas case Mandinka slave raiders from Gambia travelled sailed south capturing them, later on Jolas would become prepared for this and in turn captured Mandinkas (p. 208).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The Bijagos were too far from the mainland, coupled with their ferocious nature, proclivity to commit suicide, stage rebellions on ships, and tendency to escape plantations meant the Europeans did not favour them as slaves unless as children (p. 218).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The inter-ethnic wars were noted to never be about territorial gain or political dominance, and only rarely happened because of any real animosity (p. 208).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The incentive of European goods fuelled these wars, that European observers from the time deemed little more than robberies and man-hunts (p. 209).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Slave raiding in this way started to take the form of a profession, individuals would wholly dedicate themselves to the act of capturing slaves for profit, the heir of the throne of the Kingdom of Bissau in the 1600s was himself a professional slave raider (p. 210).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The slave trade and class divisions were intertwined in the region, victims of the slave trade were mostly of the lower class, the laws that could get individuals enslaved were created and implemented by nobles and king, this was summarised by Mateo de Anguiano:
</p><p>"the rich and powerful enjoy the privilege of making captives, because there is nobody to resist them. They (the nobles) look upon so many persons with dislike, and when they feel so inclined, they easily exercise their privilege, because their own interests are not harmed by their greed. The king proceeds with the same licence." (p. 228).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>If captured nobles were likely to be released, as the captors would be paid a ransom for them, even the Bijagos were willing to offer back captured nobles for a price, and this practice continued until the end of the 18th century, where captured nobles would be released on payment of a ransom (p. 229).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> This practice of nobles escaping slavery even carried over to the Europeans, when nobles were captured they were usually released once their nobility was discovered (p. 230). The relationship between kings and European traders was one of partnership and cooperation, with the two regularly making deals on how the trade was to be conducted, who was to be enslaved and who was not, and the prices of the slaves (pp. 230, 233, 234).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> It was noted by Fernão Guerreiro and Mateo de Anguiano when they questioned multiple kings on their part in the slave trade, they recognised the trade as evil but reasoned that they did it because the Europeans would buy no other goods from them (p. 234).<sup id="cite_ref-:4_20-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:4-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> The trade would carry on until the 19th century when it was abolished. 
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Summary">Summary</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Guinea-Bissau&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Summary">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Guinea-Bissau was once part of the kingdom of <a href="/wiki/Kaabu" title="Kaabu">Kaabu</a>, part of the <a href="/wiki/Mali_Empire" title="Mali Empire">Mali Empire</a> in the 16th century.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> Parts of this kingdom persisted until the 18th century. Other parts of the territory in the current country were considered by the Portuguese as part of <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Empire" title="Portuguese Empire">their empire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template noprint noexcerpt Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:NOTRS" class="mw-redirect" title="Wikipedia:NOTRS"><span title="This claim needs references to better sources.
